Problem sheet 6 · Equilibrium, kinetics and irreversibility

Separate a chemical equilibrium constant from a thermal relaxation coefficient and quantify entropy changes in a finite system.

Change the gas volume

At fixed temperature an ideal dimerization \(2A\rightleftharpoons A_2\) has \(K=2.00\). At total pressure \(p^\circ\) find the bound-unit fraction and dimer mole fraction. Immediately expand isothermally until all original partial pressures have halved. Find the instantaneous reaction quotient and the preferred direction.

Show the composition and direction

With bound-unit fraction \(\alpha\), equilibrium gives \(1-\alpha=(1+4Kp/p^\circ)^{-1/2}=1/3\). Hence \(\alpha=2/3\) and the dimer mole fraction \(\alpha/(2-\alpha)=1/2\). Because \(Q=a_{A_2}/a_A^2\), halving both partial pressures doubles \(Q\) from 2 to 4 before chemical readjustment. Thus \(\Delta_rG=RT\ln(Q/K)>0\), favouring dissociation. Continuing mechanical readjustment depends on whether volume or pressure is subsequently held fixed.

Two equal heat capacities

Two insulated blocks each have \(C=200\) J/K and start at 320 and 280 K. Find the common temperature, total entropy gain and initial entropy-production rate for conductance 1.50 W/K. What is their temperature difference after 50.0 s?

Show the balance and rate

The final temperature is 300 K. Entropy gain is \(200\ln[300^2/(320\times280)]=0.891\) J/K. Initial current is \(1.50(40)=60.0\) W and initial entropy production is \(60(1/280-1/320)=0.0268\) W/K. With equal capacities \(\tau=C/(2G)=66.7\) s. The difference after 50.0 s is \(40e^{-0.75}=18.9\) K. The final entropy gain depends only on endpoints, while initial rate and time history depend on conductance.

Determine whether a drift is heat leakage

A trace from two strongly insulated bodies has equal capacities of 150 J/K. Initially the measured temperatures are 330 and 290 K. After the difference has vanished both read 309 K. Estimate the apparent net heat exchange with the surroundings. Can increasing internal contact conductance explain it? Give an independent instrumental diagnosis.

Show the energy inventory

For equal capacities the initial arithmetic mean is 310 K and must stay fixed under pure internal heat exchange. Final readings imply \(\Delta U=150(309-330)+150(309-290)=-300\) J. Finite contact conductance can change the approach rate, but its internal current cancels in the total energy balance. A common thermometer drift of \(-1\) K between beginning and end would create the same apparent loss. Calibration against an independent temperature reference, or an enclosure heat-flux measurement, distinguishes these possibilities.

Extract work from a finite hot body

A body of constant heat capacity 1.00 kJ/K cools from 500 K to 300 K. A large reservoir stays at 300 K. Find the maximum work recoverable, the heat delivered to the reservoir and the entropy generated by direct thermal contact. Then predict how much of the available work remains if cooling stops at 400 K.

Worked solution

For a reversible sequence of small engines the instantaneous efficiency is 1 − T₀/T, where T is the body's current temperature. The body supplies −C dT on cooling, so Wₘₐₓ = C[(Tᵢ − T₀) − T₀ ln(Tᵢ/T₀)] = 46.75 kJ. Applying the initial Carnot efficiency to the entire 200 kJ would overestimate the work because the hot-side temperature falls.

The remaining 153.25 kJ enters the reservoir. Its entropy gain is 153.25/300 = 0.5108 kJ/K, equal to the magnitude of C ln(300/500) lost by the body. With direct contact, the reservoir instead receives all 200 kJ and total entropy production is 0.6667 − 0.5108 = 0.1558 kJ/K. Multiplication by 300 K recovers the 46.75 kJ of lost work.

At 400 K the body still has C[100 − 300 ln(4/3)] = 13.70 kJ available relative to the reservoir. The reversible work already extracted is therefore 33.05 kJ. Additivity of these two stages provides a check on both the logarithm and the choice of reference temperature.

Identify a heat capacity and a bridge conductance

Two insulated blocks start at 360 and 300 K. The cooler block has heat capacity 1500 J/K. The capacity-weighted equilibrium temperature is 320 K, and a fitted temperature difference decays by a factor \(e\) in 125 s. Infer the unknown hot-block heat capacity and the conductance. Predict the two observables after doubling that conductance in the downloadable model.

Work through the solution

Energy conservation gives \(C_h(360-320)=1500(320-300)\), hence \(C_h=750\) J/K. Subtracting the two block balances gives \(1/\tau=G(1/C_h+1/C_c)\). Therefore \(G=[125(1/750+1/1500)]^{-1}=4.00\) W/K. Doubling \(G\) to 8.00 W/K gives 62.5 s and leaves the final temperature at 320 K. Equilibrium data alone would identify the heat-capacity ratio while leaving the conductance undetermined.

Use the OneSimulation laboratory to check your answer

Chapter 13 · How a finite heat bath changes a fluctuation

A tiny sample exchanges heat with its mounting plate inside an insulated chamber. A thermometer samples the same mean temperature whether the plate is large or small. Can the time record of the sample energy reveal the size of the plate?

Count the states compatible with one reading

Call the two bodies A and B. Fix total energy \(U\), volume and particle numbers. Assume weak thermal contact so energies add, although exchange occurs over the observation time. Divide energy into small equal bins wide enough to contain many microstates. If the sample has energy \(E\), the plate must have \(U-E\). Under the microcanonical equal-weight hypothesis the probability of this bin is proportional to the product of the two state counts. Introduce \(S=k_B\ln\Omega\). Maximising probability is now equivalent to maximising a sum.

\[k_B\ln P(E)=S_A(E)+S_B(U-E)+\text{constant}.\]

Moving energy from B to A increases the first entropy with slope \(1/T_A\) and decreases the second with slope \(1/T_B\). At an interior maximum their temperatures therefore agree. This argument determines the position of the peak. Its width requires one more derivative. At fixed volume \(dT/dE=1/C\), so differentiating \(1/T\) with respect to energy gives \(-1/(T^2 C)\). Differentiating the plate's entropy twice brings two factors of minus one from its argument. Its curvature consequently enters with the same sign as the sample curvature.

\[\ln\frac{P(E_*+\delta E)}{P(E_*)}\simeq-\frac{(\delta E)^2}{2k_BT^2}\left(\frac1{C_A}+\frac1{C_B}\right).\]

For positive heat capacities the peak is stable. Match this expression to the exponent of a Gaussian to identify \(\sigma_E^2=k_BT^2 C_A C_B/(C_A+C_B)\). The reciprocal capacities add because heating A also cools B. A genuinely macroscopic plate changes temperature so little that its reciprocal capacity disappears. The remaining formula then agrees with the canonical variance.

Two calorimeters at the same temperature

Measure energies in \(k_BT\) and capacities in \(k_B\). Let A have capacity 60. An effectively infinite reservoir gives a standard deviation \(\sqrt{60}=7.75\). A plate with equal capacity gives \(\sqrt{30}=5.48\). A plate with capacity 20 gives \(\sqrt{15}=3.87\). Doubling the width would quadruple the variance. These distinctions matter if an energy histogram is used to estimate heat capacity. In the equal-capacity case, applying the canonical formula would yield 30 instead of the actual 60.

A second consequence concerns correlations. Within this isolated system a fluctuation of \(+3k_BT\) in A forces a change of \(-3k_BT\) in B. Their covariance equals \(-\sigma_E^2\). The temperatures consequently fluctuate in opposite directions by amounts inversely proportional to each body's capacity. Repeated measurements must be spaced by the relaxation time if their scatter is to estimate an equilibrium variance with independent samples.

Compare two normalised histograms

Keep the sample capacity fixed while reducing the reservoir capacity. A narrower curve must grow taller to retain unit integrated probability. Locate the displacement at which its height is about 61 percent of its maximum. This displacement measures one standard deviation directly in the horizontal units. The quadratic expansion models the central peak. Energy bounds, strong coupling or a heat capacity varying substantially across the peak require the original entropy functions.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on inferring a heat capacity

A sample with heat capacity \(90k_B\) has a measured standard deviation of \(6k_BT\). Infer the plate capacity and predict the scatter when the original plate is replaced with two identical plates in thermal contact.

Work through the solution

The measured variance in thermal units is 36. Write \(1/36=1/90+1/c_B\) to obtain \(c_B=60\). Two plates give 120. The new variance is \(90(120)/210=51.43\), so the standard deviation is \(7.17k_BT\). Increasing the plate capacity moves the result towards the canonical limit \(\sqrt{90}k_BT\).

A calorimeter measures both bodies simultaneously. Its inferred temperature deviations have slope \(\delta T_B/\delta T_A=-3\). Combined with an energy variance \(15(k_BT)^2\), determine both capacities.

Work through the solution

Energy conservation gives \(\delta T_B/\delta T_A=-C_A/C_B\), hence \(C_A=3C_B\). The effective dimensionless capacity is \(3c_B/4=15\). Thus \(C_B=20k_B\) and \(C_A=60k_B\). A covariance measurement and a histogram together identify both parameters where a width alone would give only their harmonic combination.

How would halving both heat capacities change their energy scatter and their temperature scatter at unchanged \(T\)?

Work through the solution

The effective capacity scales by one half, so \(\sigma_E\) decreases by \(1/\sqrt2\). Each temperature scatter is \(\sigma_E/C\) and increases by \(\sqrt2\). Smaller thermal systems can exhibit smaller absolute energy excursions and larger temperature excursions simultaneously.

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, fundamentals and ensembles · Fließbach, Statistische Physik.

Chapter 14 · Counting occupied sites on a surface

A surface with \(M\) identical sites exchanges particles with a gas. As the gas chemical potential rises, the coverage grows and eventually levels off. The same measurement also predicts the magnitude of number fluctuations, without estimating a time-dependent sticking rate.

Start with one site and a reservoir

An empty site has energy zero and population zero. An occupied site has energy \(-\epsilon\) with \(\epsilon>0\), and population one. Assume each site can bind at most one particle, the sites do not interact, and both states have unit degeneracy. A reservoir that supplies heat and particles assigns a state weight proportional to \(\exp[-\beta(E-\mu N)]\), where \(\beta=1/(k_BT)\). One may recover the combination \(E-\mu N\) by expanding the reservoir entropy after removing energy \(E\) and particles \(N\), using \(T\,dS=dU-\mu\,dN\) at fixed volume.

\[\Xi_1=1+e^{\beta(\mu+\epsilon)},\]
\[\theta=P(1)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\beta(\mu+\epsilon)}}.\]

At \(\mu=-\epsilon\) empty and occupied are equally likely. A more negative chemical potential penalises bringing a particle out of the gas. Positive binding energy counteracts that penalty. The reference for both energies must be the same. For an ideal dilute gas at fixed temperature, changing the pressure from \(p_1\) to \(p_2\) changes the chemical potential by \(k_BT\ln(p_2/p_1)\). Thus doubling the pressure doubles the odds \(\theta/(1-\theta)\), even when the coverage itself grows only slightly.

Factorisation explains the noise

With independent sites every configuration has a product of one-site weights, so summing over all binary choices gives \(\Xi=\Xi_1^M\). Alternatively group configurations by population \(N\). There are \(\binom{M}{N}\) ways to choose the occupied sites. Division by \(\Xi\) gives the binomial probability \(P(N)=\binom{M}{N}\theta^N(1-\theta)^{M-N}\). Since a binary variable satisfies \(n^2=n\), its variance is \(\theta-\theta^2\). Independent sites have zero cross-covariance. Adding their variances gives

\[\langle N\rangle=M\theta,\]
\[\operatorname{var}N=M\theta(1-\theta)=k_BT\left(\frac{\partial\langle N\rangle}{\partial\mu}\right)_T.\]

For \(M=100\) at half coverage the mean is 50 and the standard deviation is 5 particles. Raising the odds to 9 gives mean 90 and deviation 3. On the steep part of the isotherm small changes of reservoir conditions produce the largest response. Thermal noise is largest there for precisely the same reason. A broad distribution near a sharp steepening can also signal collective behaviour, but the independent-site prediction provides a quantitative comparison before invoking interactions.

Measure a shift in binding energy

Imagine comparing two surfaces in the same chamber. Their temperature and chemical potential agree. If the second binds particles more strongly by \(k_BT\ln4\), it multiplies the odds by four. Where the first has coverage \(1/5\), its odds are \(1/4\), so the second reaches exactly one half. Move the binding-energy slider and identify the chemical potential at the centre of the curve. The plot also displays the number variance per site on the same dimensionless scale. At very small coverage the two curves nearly coincide because \(\theta^2\) becomes negligible.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on recovering an isotherm

At pressure \(p_0\) a surface has mean coverage 0.75. How much must pressure change for coverage 0.90 at the same temperature? For 400 sites, calculate the initial and final root-mean-square fluctuations in coverage.

Work through the solution

The odds change from 3 to 9, requiring \(p=3p_0\). The variance of the fractional coverage \(N/M\) is \(\theta(1-\theta)/M\). Taking square roots gives 0.0217 initially and 0.0150 finally. These numbers measure instantaneous equilibrium fluctuations. The uncertainty of a time average additionally depends on how long successive readings remain correlated.

Infer two independent predictions from the probability of a completely empty three-site patch at half coverage. What fraction of frames show at least two occupied sites?

Work through the solution

Empty probability is \((1/2)^3=1/8\). Two occupied sites have probability \(\binom32/8=3/8\), and three have probability \(1/8\), giving one half for at least two. Non-binomial counts at fixed mean are a direct test of equivalence and independence of the sites.

Suppose every occupied site admits two internal states of equal energy while an empty site has only one. Determine the shift of the half-coverage chemical potential.

Work through the solution

The one-site grand sum becomes \(1+2e^{\beta(\mu+\epsilon)}\). Half coverage requires the occupied and empty weights to agree, so \(\mu=-\epsilon-k_BT\ln2\). Internal entropy shifts the measured midpoint without changing the exclusion of a second particle.

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, grand canonical ensemble and quantum gases.

Chapter 15 · How a pair potential changes the gas pressure

A low-density gas can measure its own intermolecular forces. At a prescribed temperature the first deviation from ideal behaviour is a volume. Its sign tells us whether overlap exclusion or attraction makes the larger contribution.

Separate independent pairs from correlated pairs

Let \(N\) classical particles occupy volume \(V\), with a total potential energy equal to a sum of short-range pair potentials \(u(r_{ij})\). Momentum integrals give the ideal-gas factors. In the remaining positional integral write each pair's Boltzmann weight as \(e^{-\beta u}=1+f(r)\). The auxiliary function \(f=e^{-\beta u}-1\) vanishes where particles do not interact. In a large vessel the positional integral with one \(f\) is \(V^{N-1}\int f(\boldsymbol r)\,d^3r\). One particle of the pair may be placed anywhere and its neighbour's relative coordinate is sampled against \(f\). There are \(N(N-1)/2\) possible unordered pairs.

In the low-density expansion of \(\ln Z\), independent repetitions of separate pairs cancel against products from the logarithm. The first connected correction is \(N(N-1)\int f/(2V)\). This is a density expansion of the free energy per particle. The total correction need not be small for a macroscopic sample. Differentiating with \(p=k_BT(\partial\ln Z/\partial V)_{T,N}\) supplies a minus sign from the reciprocal volume. In the thermodynamic limit with number density \(\rho=N/V\),

\[\frac{p}{\rho k_BT}=1+B_2(T)\rho+O(\rho^2),\]
\[B_2=-\tfrac12\int f\,d^3r=-2\pi\int_0^\infty f(r)r^2\,dr.\]

An impenetrable core gives \(f=-1\). An attractive annulus gives \(f>0\). The sign of \(B_2\) follows from their integrated volumes, including the spherical shell factor \(r^2\).

Integrate a square well

Set \(u=\infty\) for \(r<d\), \(u=-\epsilon\) for \(d<r<\lambda d\), and \(u=0\) outside. The two nonzero contributions are constants integrated over spherical volumes. With \(B_{\rm core}=2\pi d^3/3\), obtain

\[B_2=B_{\rm core}\{1-(\lambda^3-1)[e^{\epsilon/(k_BT)}-1]\}.\]

A sphere of diameter \(d\) has actual volume \(\pi d^3/6\). The hard-core coefficient is four times that volume. The forbidden positions concern the centre of a second particle, and the factor of one half avoids counting an interacting pair twice. For \(\lambda=1.5\) and \(k_BT/\epsilon=2\), the ratio \(B_2/B_{\rm core}=1-2.375(e^{0.5}-1)\simeq-0.541\). A dilute sample with \(\rho B_{\rm core}=0.02\) consequently has \(p/(\rho k_BT)\simeq0.9892\) at this order.

Locate the temperature at which the slope vanishes

Set \(B_2=0\) and solve for the Boyle temperature. The result is \(k_BT_B/\epsilon=1/\ln[1+1/(\lambda^3-1)]\), or 2.846 for the preceding range. Vary the width of the well. A larger attraction volume can offset the hard core even when the temperature is high. At \(T_B\) the pressure curve has zero first density correction. Three-particle contributions can still produce curvature. At very low temperatures pair binding can become strong enough that even a superficially low density fails to justify truncation.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on inferring the range

In a square-well model the measured Boyle temperature is \(k_BT_B/\epsilon=2\). Determine \(\lambda\), then predict the sign of the pressure correction when the temperature is doubled.

Work through the solution

Rearrangement gives \(\lambda^3=1+1/(e^{1/2}-1)=2.5415\), hence \(\lambda\simeq1.365\). Doubling the temperature decreases the attractive Boltzmann factor and leaves the hard-core term fixed. Substitution at reduced temperature 4 gives \(B_2/B_{\rm core}\simeq1-1.5415(0.2840)=0.562\). At sufficiently low density the pressure exceeds the ideal prediction.

Can a negative \(B_2\) at one temperature fix how much of the molecular attraction comes from its depth and how much from its range?

Work through the solution

The coefficient in the square-well model depends on the product \((\lambda^3-1)[e^{\epsilon/k_BT}-1]\). Many combinations of width and depth give the same product. Measurements at two temperatures can constrain both parameters if the core diameter is independently known and the model is appropriate. Additional temperatures expose inadequacies in the assumed well shape.

Take two measured compressibility factors \(Z=0.9950,0.9900\) at \(\rho B_{\rm core}=0.010,0.020\) at the same temperature. Estimate \(B_2/B_{\rm core}\) and check consistency of the truncation with these two measurements.

Work through the solution

The slopes from the ideal limit are \((Z-1)/(\rho B_{\rm core})=-0.50\) for both points. Their common value agrees with a leading pair correction and shows no detectable curvature at the stated precision. It provides no bound on higher-order terms outside that measured density interval.

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, classical gases and the cluster expansion.

Chapter 16 · Why two coexisting phases share a tangent

A material prepared with an intermediate composition can separate into two regions of different compositions. Its total composition is conserved. How can a free-energy graph predict both final compositions and the amount of each phase?

Minimise the free energy of a mixture

Work at fixed temperature and volume with a constant density of equivalent sites. Let \(f(c)\) be the Helmholtz free energy per site of a spatially uniform state with fraction \(c\) of species B. If the two regions have compositions \(c_1,c_2\) and the second contains fraction \(x\) of all sites, conservation requires \(\bar c=(1-x)c_1+xc_2\). Neglect interface energy per site in the macroscopic limit. The mixture free energy per site is then the weighted sum of the two \(f\)'s, a point on the chord joining them.

Introduce a multiplier \(\mu\) and minimise \((1-x)f(c_1)+xf(c_2)-\mu[(1-x)c_1+xc_2-\bar c]\). Varying \(c_1,c_2\) when both regions are present gives \(f'(c_1)=f'(c_2)=\mu\). This derivative measures the chemical work to replace an A particle by B. Varying \(x\) supplies the missing condition,

\[f(c_2)-f(c_1)=\mu(c_2-c_1).\]

The common slope therefore equals the slope of the connecting chord. A straight line must touch the free energy at both endpoints and lie below competing homogeneous states. Equivalently \(\int_{c_1}^{c_2}[f'(c)-\mu]\,dc=0\). This equal-area condition is the composition version of Maxwell's construction. For liquids plotted as pressure versus specific volume, the conjugate slope acquires the sign from \(p=-\partial F/\partial V\).

Find the coexistence and stability boundaries separately

Take \(f(c)=A[(c-m)^2-d^2]^2+\mu_0c\), with \(A>0\), \(m=0.5\) and \(0<d<0.5\). Subtracting the linear term tilts all trial chords by the same amount. At \(c=m\pm d\) the remaining term vanishes with zero derivative. Hence the common tangent is \(\mu_0 c\), and the coexistence compositions are \(m-d,m+d\). At \(d=0.30\) a sample of average composition 0.45 separates into 0.20 and 0.80 regions. Conservation gives \(x=(0.45-0.20)/(0.80-0.20)=5/12\) for the B-rich region.

Now perturb a uniform state into two equal parts at \(c\pm\eta\). Their average energy differs from the uniform energy by \(f''(c)\eta^2/2+O(\eta^4)\). Here \(f''=4A[3(c-m)^2-d^2]\). Between \(m-d/\sqrt3\) and \(m+d/\sqrt3\) infinitesimal fluctuations reduce the energy. Between either of these limits and the nearest coexistence endpoint a uniform state resists small fluctuations but a sufficiently large rearrangement can still lower the energy. This accounts for metastability and for the distinction between spinodal and coexistence boundaries. The uniform model does not predict a nucleation time because interfaces carry no energy in this construction.

Get a phase fraction from a graph

The display subtracts \(\mu_0c\) and measures energies in \(A\). Move the mean composition across the whole interval. Outside the coexistence window the stable composition is homogeneous. Inside, the endpoints stay put while the rich-region fraction changes linearly. Compare \(c=0.45\) to \(c=0.55\) for the same gap. Reflection symmetry exchanges the two phase fractions and leaves the free-energy saving unchanged.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on a composition quench

A homogeneous sample is prepared at \(c=0.26\) with \(d=0.30\). Does an infinitesimal composition fluctuation lower its energy? What fraction of the final mixture occupies the B-rich phase in equilibrium?

Work through the solution

The lower spinodal lies at \(0.5-0.3/\sqrt3=0.327\), so the prepared state lies below it and has positive curvature. Nevertheless 0.26 lies between 0.20 and 0.80. Its equilibrium rich fraction is \(0.06/0.60=0.10\). Local stability and the global free-energy minimum answer different experimental questions.

For \(m=0.50\), \(d=0.30\), compare free-energy gains of demixing at mean compositions 0.20 and 0.50 after subtracting the tilt. Report the gain in units of \(A\).

Work through the solution

At a coexistence endpoint the pure homogeneous material already lies on the common tangent and has zero gain. At the midpoint the homogeneous energy exceeds the tangent by \(d^4 A=0.0081A\) per site. The equilibrium mixture uses equal amounts of the two compositions. The gain tends continuously to zero at either endpoint.

Would adding \(b+mc\) to the free energy change the two selected compositions or spinodals? What would change in the tangent construction?

Work through the solution

Conservation fixes the mean composition, so the new term contributes \(b+m\bar c\) to every candidate mixture at that mean. The minimiser is unchanged. Second derivatives are unchanged, preserving spinodals. Both endpoint tangent slopes, and thus the chemical-potential difference assigned to coexistence, shift by \(m\).

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, phase equilibrium and Maxwell construction.

Chapter 17 · Extracting a length from thermal spin fluctuations

A one-dimensional row of magnetic moments favours local alignment. At low temperature a snapshot can contain a long row of identical spins. An exact calculation distinguishes a large correlated domain from a thermodynamic phase with permanent order.

Sum all configurations by multiplying a small matrix

Consider \(N\) classical spins \(s_j=\pm1\) on a ring with \(s_{N+1}=s_1\). At zero applied field the energy is \(-J\sum_j s_js_{j+1}\) with \(J>0\). The Boltzmann weight factors into nearest-neighbour terms \(e^{Ks_js_{j+1}}\), with \(K=J/(k_BT)\). Construct the matrix \(T_{ss'}=e^{Kss'}\), whose diagonal elements are \(e^K\) and off-diagonal elements \(e^{-K}\). Multiplying \(T\)'s automatically sums each intermediate spin over its two choices. Closing the first and last indices gives \(Z_N=\operatorname{tr}(T^N)\).

The symmetric and antisymmetric vectors \((1,1)/\sqrt2\) and \((1,-1)/\sqrt2\) have eigenvalues \(\lambda_+=2\cosh K\) and \(\lambda_-=2\sinh K\). Thus \(Z_N=\lambda_+^N+\lambda_-^N\). At every finite positive temperature the ratio of eigenvalues is strictly below one. In the large-system limit the free energy per spin is \(-k_BT\ln(2\cosh K)\). Differentiating with respect to \(\beta\) yields the energy per spin \(-J\tanh K\).

Insert two local measurements

Multiplying an individual configuration by a measured spin corresponds to inserting \(S=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)\) in the matrix trace. To measure two sites separated by \(r\) bonds use \(\operatorname{tr}(ST^rST^{N-r})/Z_N\). In the eigenbasis of \(T\), \(S\) exchanges the symmetric and antisymmetric vectors. The numerator therefore contains one factor \(\lambda_-^r\lambda_+^{N-r}\) and one with the eigenvalues exchanged. With \(q=\lambda_-/\lambda_+=\tanh K\),

\[\langle s_0s_r\rangle_N=\frac{q^r+q^{N-r}}{1+q^N},\]
\[\langle s_0s_r\rangle_\infty=q^r=e^{-ra/\xi},\]
\[\xi=-\frac{a}{\ln q}.\]

Here \(a\) is the lattice spacing and the infinite-chain limit holds for fixed \(r\). The second finite-ring term comes from the other way around the circle. At equal sites the full ratio equals one. In zero field spin reversal makes the mean spin zero in the thermal distribution. Thus this correlation already equals the connected correlation.

Compare a chain at two temperatures

At \(k_BT=J\), \(q=0.7616\) and \(\xi=3.67a\). Spins ten bonds apart have correlation only \(0.0657\). At \(k_BT=0.5J\), \(\xi=27.30a\) and the corresponding correlation is \(0.693\). Lower temperature suppresses individual domain walls. Since \(s_js_{j+1}\) is +1 for alignment and −1 for a wall, its average equals \(1-2P_{\rm wall}=q\). At low temperature \(P_{\rm wall}\simeq e^{-2K}\) and \(\xi/a\simeq e^{2K}/2\). Even rare walls eventually destroy alignment in a sufficiently long chain.

Compare the finite ring with the infinite-chain result. Change the ring length while leaving the temperature fixed. When the correlation length becomes comparable to the circumference, the long route contributes appreciably. Returning to the starting site must restore correlation one. A straight-line fit to the back half of a semilog finite-ring plot would consequently give a misleading decay length.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on estimating an interaction

In a very long zero-field chain the nearest-neighbour correlation is 0.6. Determine \(J/(k_BT)\), the probability of a wall on a given bond and the separation at which correlation first falls below 0.1.

Work through the solution

Invert \(q=\tanh K\) to obtain \(K=\tfrac12\ln[(1+q)/(1-q)]=0.6931\). The wall probability is \((1-q)/2=0.20\). Solve \(r>\ln(0.1)/\ln(0.6)=4.51\). At five bonds the correlation is 0.07776. An even number of walls can restore the original spin direction, so this correlation counts more than the probability of finding a completely wall-free interval.

At \(k_BT=J\) a nearest-neighbour bond is aligned with probability \(p\). Find \(p\) and its relation to the mean energy per bond.

Work through the solution

The correlation is \(2p-1=\tanh1=0.7616\), so \(p=0.8808\). Multiplying \(2p-1\) by \(-J\) gives \(-0.7616J\). This directly connects counts of local defects in snapshots with the thermodynamic energy obtained from the partition function.

Why can an odd number of walls never occur on a closed ring? Would dropping the finite-ring correction give the correct wall count at arbitrary temperature?

Work through the solution

Each wall reverses the spin when following the ring. Returning to the starting site requires an even number of reversals. The exact nearest-neighbour correlation is \((q+q^{N-1})/(1+q^N)\). Thus the mean wall count is \(N(1-C_1)/2\). Approximating \(C_1\) by \(q\) works when \(N\) substantially exceeds the correlation length; otherwise ring closure changes the result.

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, Ising chain and correlation functions · Cambridge statistical mechanics, phase transitions and transfer matrices.

Chapter 18 · Why only a narrow shell of a Fermi gas carries heat

Heating a filled Fermi sea does not give every particle a classical thermal energy. Deep below the chemical potential, nearby states are already occupied. Quantifying the accessible states predicts both the low-temperature heat capacity and the shift of the chemical potential at fixed particle number.

Convert a sharp Fermi surface into a narrow weight

For independent fermions with occupancy \(f(E)=1/[1+\exp((E-\mu)/(k_BT))]\), consider integrals \(I=\int_0^\infty H(E)f(E)\,dE\). Choose \(F(E)=\int_0^E H(s)ds\). Integration by parts gives \(I=\int_0^\infty F(E)[-\partial_E f(E)]dE\), since the boundary terms vanish. The derivative is a peak of width \(k_BT\) around \(\mu\). With \(x=(E-\mu)/(k_BT)\) the weight becomes \(w(x)=1/[4\cosh^2(x/2)]\).

For \(\mu\) many thermal units above the bottom and \(H\) smooth over the thermal shell, extend the lower limit to \(-\infty\) when expanding locally around \(\mu\). The weight has unit integral and is even, so the linear term in \(F(\mu+k_BTx)\) integrates to zero. Its second moment is \(\pi^2/3\), obtained by expanding \(w(x)=\sum_{\ell\ge1}(-1)^{\ell+1}\ell e^{-\ell x}\) on \(x>0\) and integrating \(x^2\) termwise. Since \(F''=H'\),

\[I=\int_0^\mu H(E)dE+\frac{\pi^2}{6}(k_BT)^2H'(\mu)+O(T^4).\]

The first correction samples the slope of the smooth weighting function. If a band edge or singularity lies within the shell, replacing the lower limit and Taylor expanding are poor approximations.

Conserve the number of particles

For a three-dimensional parabolic dispersion the density of states is \(D(E)=A\sqrt E\). At \(T=0\), \(N=(2A/3)E_F^{3/2}\). At small \(T\), use the expansion with \(H=D\) and write the chemical potential as \(E_F+\delta\mu\), where \(\delta\mu=O(T^2)\). Subtract the zero-temperature count. To quadratic order \(0=D(E_F)\delta\mu+(\pi^2/6)(k_BT)^2D'(E_F)\). Because \(D'/D=1/(2E_F)\),

\[\mu/E_F=1-\frac{\pi^2}{12}(T/T_F)^2+O[(T/T_F)^4],\]
\[T_F=E_F/k_B.\]

The increasing density of states offers more available levels just above the Fermi energy than just below it. The chemical potential shifts down to preserve the count. At \(T/T_F=0.10\) the predicted shift is \(-0.00822E_F\). For an ideal gas with \(E_F=5.0\,\mathrm{eV}\), this particular reduced temperature corresponds to about 5800 K, so reduced temperature and ordinary laboratory temperature should be distinguished.

Differentiate the energy at fixed number

Use \(H=ED\) in the same integral, including the just-derived shift of \(\mu\). The boundary shift contributes \(E_FD(E_F)\delta\mu\). In the Sommerfeld term the derivative is \(D(E_F)+E_FD'(E_F)\). The terms involving the slope of \(D\) cancel after fixing number. Thus \(U-U_0=(\pi^2/6)D(E_F)(k_BT)^2\), and \(C_V=(\pi^2/3)D(E_F)k_B^2T\). In three dimensions \(D(E_F)=3N/(2E_F)\), giving \(C_V/(Nk_B)=(\pi^2/2)T/T_F\). This is an electronic contribution for independent particles. Other degrees of freedom such as phonons can dominate a measured material heat capacity.

The experiment reconstructs \(\mu\) by numerical integration and bisection at fixed number. It overlays the asymptotic prediction, allowing the fractional error to be checked before using it at larger \(T/T_F\). The quadrature uses the momentum-like substitution \(E/E_F=y^2\) to remove the square-root endpoint singularity.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on interpreting heat-capacity data

For a gas with \(E_F=4.0\,\mathrm{eV}\) at \(T=300\,\mathrm K\), estimate \(C_V/(Nk_B)\) and the chemical-potential change in eV. Use \(k_BT=0.02585\,\mathrm{eV}\).

Work through the solution

The reduced temperature is \(0.00646\). The heat capacity ratio is approximately \(0.0319\), and \(\delta\mu=-(\pi^2/12)(0.02585)^2/4.0=-1.37\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{eV}\).

A two-dimensional parabolic band has constant density of states above its edge. What does the quadratic expansion predict for the fixed-number chemical potential and energy?

Work through the solution

\(D'=0\) makes the quadratic shift of \(\mu\) vanish as long as the edge is distant. The energy integral still uses \((ED)'=D\), so it has a positive \(T^2\) correction. Effects of the distant band edge can give exponentially small departures in \(\mu\).

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, degenerate Fermi gas and Sommerfeld expansion.

Chapter 19 · Mean-field ordering, response and the critical point

Interactions can make a magnet favour a nonzero moment even when the applied field vanishes. Averaging the local exchange field gives a solvable model whose critical predictions can be compared with the exact one-dimensional chain.

Build the free energy from the count of spin states

Take \(N\) spins \(s_i=\pm1\), nearest-neighbour energy \(-J\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}s_is_j\) and Zeeman energy \(-h\sum_i s_i\), where \(h\) has energy units. Denote mean spin by \(m\). The fractions of positive and negative spins are \(p_\pm=(1\pm m)/2\). The binomial count and Stirling's approximation give entropy per spin \(-k_B(p_+\ln p_++p_-\ln p_-)\). Independent-neighbour mean field replaces each pair average by \(m^2\). With \(z\) neighbours and each bond counted once, interaction energy per spin becomes \(-zJm^2/2\).

\[f(m)=-\tfrac12zJm^2-hm+k_BT[p_+\ln p_++p_-\ln p_-].\]

Differentiate at fixed \(T,h\). The entropy contribution has derivative \(k_BT\operatorname{atanh}m\), giving \(m=\tanh[(zJm+h)/(k_BT)]\). This equation can have three real roots, so an iterative solver returning a root alone is insufficient. The equilibrium phase minimises \(f\); the second derivative \(k_BT/(1-m^2)-zJ\) distinguishes local minima and maxima.

Expand about the loss of stability

Near zero magnetisation, \(\operatorname{atanh}m=m+m^3/3+\cdots\). Set \(T_c=zJ/k_B\) and integrate the expanded derivative to obtain

\[f(m)-f(0)\simeq\tfrac{k_B}{2}(T-T_c)m^2+\tfrac{k_BT}{12}m^4-hm .\]

At zero field and \(T>T_c\), \(m=0\) is stable. Below \(T_c\), symmetry gives two minima whose leading magnitudes satisfy \(m^2\simeq3(T_c-T)/T\). Thus the order parameter vanishes like \((T_c-T)^{1/2}\) close to \(T_c\). At \(T=T_c\) a small nonzero field instead gives \(m\simeq[3h/(k_BT_c)]^{1/3}\). These powers describe this model's limiting behaviour. For a finite sample symmetry makes the unbiased ensemble average zero if it samples both minima. Spontaneous magnetisation refers to the thermodynamic limit with an infinitesimal field selecting a sign.

Compute a response, then compare with an exact counterexample

Above \(T_c\) the small-field stationary equation gives \(\chi=\partial m/\partial h=1/[k_B(T-T_c)]\). Below \(T_c\), differentiate the exact stationarity equation on the positive branch, obtaining \(\chi=[k_BT/(1-m^2)-zJ]^{-1}\). Expanding near \(T_c\) there gives \(\chi\simeq[2k_B(T_c-T)]^{-1}\), half the above-transition amplitude at equal distance. At \(T=0.8T_c\), the full zero-field solution is about \(m=0.7104\); the small-\(m\) estimate \(\sqrt{3(1-0.8)/0.8}=0.866\) is already visibly inaccurate.

Mean field predicts an ordering temperature even for a one-dimensional chain with \(z=2\). The exact finite-temperature correlation length in the earlier transfer-matrix solution is finite at all \(T>0\). Spatial fluctuations and domain walls invalidate the independent-neighbour closure in one dimension. The experiment plots the full mean-field free energy and its quartic approximation for the same normalised \(T,h\). Find the minima before inspecting the calculated order parameter. Compare how a weak bias changes their relative depths.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on critical response

At \(T=1.10T_c\), what is the linear-response mean spin expected at \(h/(k_BT_c)=0.002\)? At the critical point use the cubic equation to estimate \(m\) for the same field.

Work through the solution

Above the transition \(m\simeq0.002/0.10=0.020\). At criticality \(m\simeq(0.006)^{1/3}=0.182\). The larger response signals the loss of the quadratic restoring term; linear extrapolation through \(T_c\) would give a divergent susceptibility.

At \(h=0\) and \(T=0.95T_c\), is \(m=0\) a possible output from a fixed-point solver? How should an equilibrium calculation prevent a wrong interpretation?

Work through the solution

Starting the symmetric iteration at exactly zero returns zero repeatedly, even below \(T_c\). Its free-energy curvature is \(k_B(T-T_c)<0\); it is a local maximum. Compare the free energies or bracket the two symmetry-related nonzero stationary points before reporting an equilibrium moment.

Further reading · Cambridge statistical physics, mean-field Ising and Landau theory.

Problem sheet 7 · Can a fluctuation reveal attraction on a surface?

Two surfaces have identical mean coverage at a chosen gas pressure. Repeated microscopy images nevertheless show different event-to-event scatter in the number of occupied sites. Determine whether interactions can account for the excess and design a measurement that distinguishes clustering from instrumental drift.

Predict the independent-site baseline

Each image samples 100 equivalent sites in thermal equilibrium. At half coverage, predict the mean occupied population, its standard deviation and the slope of the mean population with respect to \(\beta\mu\). What changes if independent sites have binding energies larger by \(k_BT\ln3\) at fixed \(\mu\)?

Work through the solution

At \(\theta=1/2\), mean population is 50 and standard deviation \(\sqrt{100/4}=5\). The number fluctuation identity gives slope 25. Multiplying the original odds by three gives coverage \(3/4\), mean 75, variance \(100(3/4)(1/4)=18.75\) and standard deviation 4.33. A change of mean binding at fixed coverage, compensated by the chemical potential, cannot by itself enhance the independent-site half-coverage variance.

Check the half-coverage variance and the response to a chemical-potential shift

Map an interacting row to spins

Model the surface as a ring of sites with occupancies \(n_j=0,1\), attraction \(-w n_jn_{j+1}\) per occupied pair, and grand-energy contribution \(-(\epsilon+\mu)n_j\) per site. Put \(n_j=(1+s_j)/2\). Determine the Ising exchange \(J\) and the chemical potential that gives zero Ising field.

Work through the solution

The pair product becomes \([1+s_j+s_{j+1}+s_js_{j+1}]/4\). On a periodic row each site enters two pair terms. The exchange is \(J=w/4\) and the field coefficient in \(-H\sum_j s_j\) is \(H=(w+\epsilon+\mu)/2\). Choosing \(\mu=-\epsilon-w\) gives \(H=0\), and spin reversal then enforces mean occupation \(1/2\). Pair attraction can therefore be hidden by a shift of the half-coverage chemical potential.

Use the correlation length to predict number noise

Let \(w=4k_BT\) at the adjusted half-coverage point. Calculate the correlation at one and ten bonds and the predicted population deviation for 100 sites. Compare to the independent-site prediction.

Work through the solution

Here \(J/(k_BT)=1\), \(q=\tanh1=0.76159\), and \(\langle s_0s_r\rangle\simeq q^r\), giving 0.7616 and 0.0657. Since \(n=(1+s)/2\), occupancy covariance is \(q^r/4\). Summing the covariance over all site pairs for a ring much longer than \(-1/\ln q=3.67\) gives \(\operatorname{var}N\simeq(100/4)[1+2\sum_{r=1}^\infty q^r]=25(1+q)/(1-q)=184.7\). The deviation is 13.59 sites. Positive spatial covariances account for the extra noise.

Compare a 100-site ring with a shorter ring at the same temperature

Check whether the observation is spatial or temporal

Repeat the experiment with eight sites on a ring. Derive the variance from the finite-ring correlation \(C_r=(q^r+q^{8-r})/(1+q^8)\). What measurements would test the interpretation of the excess variance on the larger sample?

Work through the solution

Translational invariance gives \(\operatorname{var}N=(8/4)\sum_{r=0}^{7} C_r=2(1+q)(1-q^8)/[(1-q)(1+q^8)]\), approximately 11.77 at \(q=\tanh1\). Report a standard deviation of about 3.43 sites. Measure occupancy covariance against spatial separation and repeat after changing the delay between images. Surface correlations predict a distance-dependent decay within individual images. Temporal drift common to all sites can instead give a substantial long-range component and serially correlated frame means.

Problem sheet 8 · Count atoms in a trap and predict the onset of condensation

A dilute cloud of bosons is confined by three harmonic restoring forces. Cooling moves particles between one-particle states while the total atom number stays constant. Start with counted lattice points in quantum-number space and predict the temperature at which the excited states can no longer hold the whole cloud.

Count levels rather than ordering them one by one

Measure energy from the single-particle ground state. Suppose the angular frequencies are \(\omega_x,\omega_y,\omega_z\). For energies far above one quantum, recover the density of levels \(g(E)\) by finding the volume of the allowed region in \((n_x,n_y,n_z)\). Check the dimensions. Should one divide by another factorial for indistinguishable atoms?

Work through the solution

Each nonnegative integer triple is a distinct one-particle state. Its excitation energy equals \(\hbar(\omega_x n_x+\omega_y n_y+\omega_z n_z)\). States below energy \(E\) fill a tetrahedron with three intercepts \(E/(\hbar\omega_i)\). Its volume is one sixth of their product. Differentiating the integrated count supplies a density per unit energy.

\[G(E)\simeq\frac{E^3}{6\hbar^3\omega_x\omega_y\omega_z},\]
\[g(E)\simeq\frac{E^2}{2(\hbar\bar\omega)^3},\]
\[\bar\omega=(\omega_x\omega_y\omega_z)^{1/3}.\]

The factor of six came from geometry. Indistinguishability enters later in the count of occupations, through the Bose distribution. Dividing the one-particle density by a factorial of atom number would change the number of available orbitals when one added a second particle. The continuous tetrahedron loses information on its faces and edges, so the estimate is best when \(k_BT\) spans many quanta in every direction.

Find how many atoms the excited cloud can hold

The Bose occupation is \(1/[\exp((E-\mu)/k_BT)-1]\). The chemical potential measured from the lowest state approaches zero from below as the population grows. Evaluate the excited-state population at this limit and determine the condensed fraction as a function of temperature. Explicitly account for the state at zero.

Work through the solution

The smooth density vanishes at zero and loses the one discrete ground orbital. Retain its population separately, \(N=N_0+N_{\rm ex}\). Insert the smooth density only for the excited contribution. Put \(x=E/(k_BT)\). The integrand contains \(x^2/(e^x-1)\). Expanding the reciprocal as \(\sum_{\ell\ge1}e^{-\ell x}\), integrate each nonnegative term. Its integral is \(2/\ell^3\). The result is

\[N_{\rm ex}^{\max}=\zeta(3)\left(\frac{k_BT}{\hbar\bar\omega}\right)^3,\]
\[T_c=\frac{\hbar\bar\omega}{k_B}\left(\frac{N}{\zeta(3)}\right)^{1/3}.\]

Below this temperature the excited cloud has the stated capacity and the balance occupies the ground state. Hence \(N_0/N=1-(T/T_c)^3\). Above \(T_c\) the chemical potential is negative and one must solve a number constraint. Extending the cubic formula above the transition would give a negative condensate fraction. This is an especially clear example of a constraint selecting which branch of a calculation has physical meaning.

A trap calibration as a numerical example

Use \(N=100000\) and cyclic frequencies \(f_x=f_y=80\,\mathrm{Hz}\), \(f_z=160\,\mathrm{Hz}\). Compute \(T_c\), the ideal fraction at 140 nK and the result of halving only the axial frequency. Distinguish the quantities kept fixed in these two comparisons.

Work through the solution

Since \(\hbar\omega=hf\), the geometric-mean cyclic frequency is \(100.79\,\mathrm{Hz}\) and \(h\bar f/k_B=4.84\,\mathrm{nK}\). With \(\zeta(3)=1.20206\) the dimensionless temperature multiplier is about 43.65. Thus \(T_c\) is about 211 nK and the fraction at 140 nK is about 0.71. Halving one frequency at fixed particle number lowers \(T_c\) by \(2^{-1/3}\) to roughly 168 nK. At the same absolute temperature the ideal fraction then falls to about 0.42. At a fixed ratio \(T/T_c\) both clouds would have the same fraction.

The same equations predict the energy below \(T_c\). Multiplying the occupation integrand by \(E\) changes the sum to \(6\zeta(4)\). Thus \(U=3\zeta(4)(k_BT)^4/(\hbar\bar\omega)^3\), measured relative to all atoms in the ground state. Differentiating at fixed trap gives \(C=12k_B\zeta(4)(k_BT/\hbar\bar\omega)^3\). The population and energy probe different moments of the same density of states. A wrong power of energy in the state count changes both predictions.

Test what a continuum approximation misses

Use the isotropic trap experiment below. For frequency \(f\), level \(n\) has energy \(nhf\). Obtain its exact degeneracy before comparing the discrete excited-state sum with the continuum. Vary \(N\) and the frequency separately. Interpret the crossing of each capacity curve with one.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Work through the solution

For each \(n_x=0,\ldots,n\), there are \(n-n_x+1\) choices of \(n_y\). Summing gives \((n+1)(n+2)/2\). The total capacity sums this degeneracy times \(1/[\exp(nhf/k_BT)-1]\) for \(n\ge1\). At a given temperature the faces and edges of the lattice supply positive state-count corrections to the leading volume estimate. Consequently the discrete capacity reaches \(N\) at a lower temperature. Increasing \(N\) makes the fractional correction smaller. Changing \(f\) rescales the temperature axis in kelvin while leaving the discrete curve versus \(T/T_c\) unchanged at fixed \(N\). The reported discrete crossing is a saturation convention for a finite ideal sample. A finite set of atoms has a smooth occupation change and interactions introduce further shifts.

Compare the trap with a gas in a box by reading the quantum-gas chapter in David Tong’s statistical physics course. Repeating the level count for a box changes the power of energy in the density of states.

Reservoirs, useful work and mixtures

Keep track of which reservoir fixes temperature, chemical potential or pressure. Close the energy and entropy balances before inferring the useful work or the coexisting phase fraction.

  4. Phase equilibrium and steam quality
  5. Gas mixtures, humidity and condensation
  6. Useful work and exergy destruction
  7. Why a real gas cools or warms across a throttle
  8. Finding the vapour fraction of an equilibrium flash
  9. When does a polar crystal change its preferred state?

State, process and the first law for a closed system

Engineering thermodynamics and heat transfer

Distinguish properties from path-dependent transfers and determine heat and work for a piston-cylinder process.

From first principles

Obtain closed-system work from the motion of a boundary

Choose a fixed mass of gas enclosed by a piston and cylinder. No mass crosses this boundary. The first law is the empirical balance of energy: with heat positive into the gas and work positive out, change of internal, kinetic and potential energy equals net heat minus net work. For a piston moving normally through \(dx\) against a uniform resisting pressure \(p_{\rm ext}\), force times displacement gives

\[\delta W_b=p_{\rm ext}A\,dx=p_{\rm ext}\,dV,\]
\[\Delta(U+KE+PE)=Q-W .\]

For slow internal equilibration with tiny pressure differences, one can use gas pressure in the integral. If bulk kinetic and potential changes are negligible and no shaft or electrical work crosses the boundary, the integrated heating requirement is

\[Q_{12}=U_2-U_1+\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p_{\rm ext}\,dV .\]

The area under the specified pressure–volume path is therefore part of the heat calculation. For a rigid vessel it vanishes. For a constant-pressure piston heating an ideal gas, \(p\Delta V=mR\Delta T\) adds to the internal-energy change. The end temperatures alone determine the ideal-gas internal-energy change, while the moving boundary determines the work.

Derivation and applications

Determine the heat input when a spring loads the piston

A freely sliding piston does not necessarily hold the gas pressure constant. Attach a linear spring to a frictionless piston of area \(A\) and let its resisting force increase as the gas expands. Consider a closed mass \(m\) of ideal gas, heated slowly enough that piston force balance holds at each intermediate state. Neglect piston kinetic energy, heat leakage and changes of elevation except as already included in the constant external pressure \(p_1\). At the initial position the gas is in equilibrium at absolute pressure \(p_1\) and volume \(V_1\).

The displacement \(x\) produces a volume increment \(Ax\) and an extra resisting force \(kx\). Dividing by piston area gives the pressure as a function of the evolving volume:

\[V-V_1=Ax,\]
\[p(V)=p_1+\frac{k}{A^2}(V-V_1).\]

Boundary work is performed at this changing external pressure. Integrating the straight-line path over the swept volume \(\Delta V=V_2-V_1\) separates work against the initial pressure from work stored in the spring:

\[W_{12}=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p(V)\,dV=p_1\Delta V+\frac{k}{2A^2}(\Delta V)^2.\]

For constant specific heat \(c_v\) and specific gas constant \(R\), the first law gives the heat required, positive into the gas. State temperatures follow from the ideal-gas relation, including the final spring pressure:

\[Q_{12}=m c_v(T_2-T_1)+W_{12},\]
\[T_i=p_iV_i/(mR).\]

Only the temperature change determines the internal-energy change; the mechanical law selects the work term. Setting \(k=0\) recovers expansion against constant pressure. Abrupt heating that accelerates the piston would invalidate the assumed quasistatic pressure path.

Worked example

Calculate the heater input for an air cylinder

Take \(m=0.0100\) kg, \(V_1=0.0100\) m³, \(p_1=100\) kPa, \(A=0.0100\) m² and \(k=1000\) N/m. Increase volume by \(0.00200\) m³. The final pressure is \(120\) kPa; the work is \((100+120)(0.00200)/2=0.220\) kJ. For \(R=0.287\) and \(c_v=0.718\) kJ/(kg K), \(\Delta T=(1.440-1.000)/(0.0100\times0.287)=153\) K. The gas receives \(1.10+0.220=1.32\) kJ of heat.

Second application

Separate actuator work from stored spring energy

On the same stroke the piston travels \(0.200\) m. The initially unloaded incremental spring stores \(kx^2/2=20.0\) J, exactly the triangular part of the pressure–volume work. The constant load receives \(200\) J. If the spring were removed while retaining the same initial state and swept volume, the final pressure would stay at \(100\) kPa and the temperature rise would be only \(69.7\) K. The two processes end at different thermodynamic states despite having the same final volume.

Decide what crosses the boundary

Draw a boundary around the gas in a piston cylinder. If no gas crosses its surface, the system is closed even though the piston moves. A rigid vessel is a special closed system with fixed volume. An insulated wall prevents heat transfer; a shaft or electrical lead can still transmit work through it. These descriptions impose different constraints and should be identified before choosing an equation.

At equilibrium a simple compressible substance can be described using independent state variables such as temperature and specific volume. Pressure \(p\), volume \(V\), temperature \(T\) and internal energy \(U\) are properties. An equation of state relates some of them. For a dilute ideal gas \(pV=mRT\), where \(R\) is the specific gas constant. Always use absolute pressure and absolute temperature in this relation. Two initial and final states determine changes in properties, but do not specify the heat and work transfers between them.

Construct the process energy balance

Take work done by the system as positive and heat entering it as positive. The first law is \(\Delta U+\Delta KE+\Delta PE=Q-W\). For a stationary vessel ignore changes in kinetic and gravitational energy of the system as a whole. A moving piston of area \(A\) against external pressure \(p_{\rm ext}\) performs incremental work \(p_{\rm ext}A\,dx=p_{\rm ext}dV\). For a quasistatic mechanically reversible motion the boundary pressure can be represented by the gas pressure throughout the process.

If gas expands from \(V_1\) to \(V_2\) at constant external pressure, \(W=p_{\rm ext}(V_2-V_1)\). If it instead follows a reversible isothermal ideal-gas path, inserting \(p=mRT/V\) and integrating gives \(W=mRT\ln(V_2/V_1)\). Both expressions have units of energy. Their different volume dependence makes a pressure-volume diagram useful: the area under each pressure path has a direct work interpretation when its boundary-force assumptions hold.

Heat air below a freely moving piston

A weight-loaded, frictionless piston keeps 0.50 kg of ideal air at 150 kPa absolute while a heater raises its temperature from 300 to 360 K. Use \(R=0.287\) and \(c_v=0.718\) kJ/(kg K), treated as constant. The initial and final volumes are \(mRT/p=0.287\) and 0.3444 m³. Boundary work is \(150(0.0574)=8.61\) kJ. For this gas the internal-energy change is \(mc_v\Delta T=0.50(0.718)(60)=21.54\) kJ.

The required heat is \(Q=\Delta U+W=30.15\) kJ. The same result follows from \(mc_p\Delta T\) because for this constant-pressure ideal-gas process with only boundary work \(c_p=c_v+R=1.005\) kJ/(kg K). The extra heat above the internal-energy increase moved the piston weight. Using \(Q=mc_p\Delta T\) without inspecting the constraint would give the wrong heat input to a rigid tank.

Compare a rigid-wall experiment

Lock the piston at its initial volume before heating an identical mass from 300 to 360 K. Now \(W=0\), so only 21.54 kJ of heat is needed. The final pressure is \(150(360/300)=180\) kPa. Equal temperature changes in an ideal gas imply equal changes in internal energy, even though final pressures and energy transfers differ. For a real fluid near a phase boundary, constant ideal-gas heat capacities may fail; use tabulated state properties or a suitable equation of state. In either case the first-law accounting is unchanged once the initial and final internal energies and the actual boundary work are known.

A different path between specified volumes

An ideal gas initially at 300 kPa and 0.020 m³ expands quasistatically and isothermally to twice its initial volume. Since \(pV=p_1V_1\), work out is \(W=p_1V_1\ln(V_2/V_1)=6\ln2=4.16\,\mathrm{kJ}\). Ideal-gas internal energy depends only on temperature, so \(\Delta U=0\) and heat entering equals 4.16 kJ. A freely expanding ideal gas in an insulated rigid outer vessel can reach the same final temperature and occupied volume while doing zero boundary work. The state variables alone do not record which process occurred.

Determine heat and work along a measured compression path

A measured pressure–volume trace contains more information than its endpoints. For some slow piston processes an empirical polytropic law \(pV^n=\text{constant}\) fits the path. Here \(n\) is a fitted process exponent and need not equal the gas heat-capacity ratio. Assume a closed cylinder with negligible kinetic and potential energy change, a quasistatic pressure load and a calorically perfect ideal gas. Work out of the gas is the area \(W=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p\,dV\), which is negative for compression. Substitute \(p=p_1V_1^n V^{-n}\) and integrate:

\[W=\frac{p_2V_2-p_1V_1}{1-n}\quad(n\ne1),\qquad W=p_1V_1\ln(V_2/V_1)\quad(n=1).\]

The logarithmic case is the continuous limit as \(n\) approaches one. The ideal-gas equation also gives \(T_2/T_1=(V_1/V_2)^{n-1}\), so \(\Delta U=mc_v(T_2-T_1)\). With \(Q-W=\Delta U\) and \(pV=mRT\), the heat input is \(Q=m[c_v+R/(1-n)](T_2-T_1)\) for \(n\ne1\). Setting \(n=\gamma=c_p/c_v\) eliminates \(Q\). This inference relies on the ideal-gas, constant-property, quasistatic assumptions. An arbitrary irreversible compression with the same endpoints cannot be called isentropic from the fitted exponent alone.

Recover a cylinder heat loss from three measurements

A test traps air at \(p_1=100\,\mathrm{kPa}\) absolute, \(V_1=0.0100\,\mathrm{m^3}\), \(T_1=300\,\mathrm K\), then compresses it to \(V_2=0.0040\,\mathrm{m^3}\). The fitted exponent is 1.25. Using \(R=0.287,\ c_v=0.718\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\), the trapped mass is \(p_1V_1/(RT_1)=0.01161\,\mathrm{kg}\). The predicted pressure is \(100(2.5)^{1.25}=314.4\,\mathrm{kPa}\), and \(T_2=300(2.5)^{0.25}=377.2\,\mathrm K\). Note that absolute pressure is required in both the fit and the gas law. A pressure transducer referenced to atmosphere would need its reference restored.

The gas does \(W=[314.4(0.0040)-100(0.0100)]/(1-1.25)=-1.03\,\mathrm{kJ}\). Its internal energy rises by \(0.01161(0.718)(77.2)=0.644\,\mathrm{kJ}\), giving \(Q=\Delta U+W=-0.386\,\mathrm{kJ}\). Compression work enters the gas while part leaves as heat through the cylinder wall. If the process were isothermal at 300 K, the same volume change would require only \(-W=0.916\,\mathrm{kJ}\) and the full amount would leave as heat. A mid-stroke measurement gives a stronger check than the end pressure: the fitted law predicts \(100(0.010/0.007)^{1.25}=156.2\,\mathrm{kPa}\) at 7.0 litres. A substantial discrepancy would favour direct numerical integration of the measured trace over using a single exponent.

An apparent exponent can also be estimated directly from pressure ratios: \(n=\ln(p_2/p_1)/\ln(V_1/V_2)\). Compare estimates from the first and second halves of the stroke. Agreement means the same log–log slope describes both halves, while disagreement may indicate changing heat transfer, leakage or phase lag between the pressure and position sensors. It does not identify which of those mechanisms is responsible. As a separate check, suppose a water jacket gains the estimated 0.386 kJ each stroke and the compressor completes ten such strokes per minute. The mean heat removal from these strokes would be \(0.386(10)/60=0.0643\,\mathrm{kW}\). A jacket measurement of the complete machine includes intake and exhaust processes and mechanical friction as well, so experimental comparison requires either an isolated compression interval or a balance around the rest of the cycle.

A change in operating speed can also change the fitted exponent because it changes the time available for heat exchange during a single compression stroke.

Open systems, enthalpy and steady-flow devices

Engineering thermodynamics and heat transfer

Use an inlet-outlet balance to distinguish shaft work, kinetic-energy conversion and pressure reduction.

From first principles

Derive the steady-flow energy balance including flow work

Surround a turbine, nozzle or mixing chamber with a stationary control surface. In addition to transporting its internal, kinetic and gravitational energies, an entering fluid is pushed across the inlet by upstream pressure. A mass increment \(dm\) occupies volume \(v\,dm\) and requires pressure work \(pv\,dm\) to enter. The outlet carries the corresponding work away. Combining internal energy \(u\) and flow work defines enthalpy:

\[h=u+pv,\]
\[e_{\rm stream}=h+V^2/2+gz .\]

Here \(V\) denotes speed, \(v\) specific volume and \(z\) elevation. In steady operation, sum these stream contributions and include heat into and shaft work out of the control volume. For one inlet and one outlet with equal mass rate, rearrangement gives

\[\dot Q-\dot W_s=\dot m\,[h_2-h_1+(V_2^2-V_1^2)/2+g(z_2-z_1)] .\]

All terms have units of power. A nozzle can accelerate flow without shaft work by lowering static enthalpy. A throttle with no heat, no shaft work and negligible speed and elevation changes is instead approximately isenthalpic. Omitting the pressure work while also using internal energy would miss both conclusions.

Derivation and applications

Convert a nozzle energy balance into a mass-flow limit

A steady nozzle converts static enthalpy into bulk kinetic energy. A converging passage has a maximum possible mass flow at a specified inlet stagnation state. Derive this for a steady, one-dimensional, adiabatic ideal gas with constant \(c_p\) and \(\gamma\), reversible acceleration and negligible inlet-reservoir velocity. Write reservoir conditions as \(T_0,p_0\) and the local static conditions as \(T,p\). With no shaft work the energy balance gives

\[c_pT_0=c_pT+u^2/2,\]
\[u=\sqrt{2c_pT_0(1-T/T_0)} .\]

Isentropic acceleration links temperature and pressure. Let \(r=T/T_0\), so \(p/p_0=r^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}\). The mass flux is \(\rho u\) and ideal-gas density is \(p/(RT)\). Substitution gives a function of just one local variable:

\[G(r)=\frac{p_0}{RT_0}\sqrt{2c_pT_0}\,r^{1/(\gamma-1)}\sqrt{1-r}.\]

To find its maximum differentiate \(\ln G=(\ln r)/(\gamma-1)+\ln(1-r)/2+\text{constant}\). Equating the two derivative terms gives the sonic temperature and pressure ratios:

\[r_*=\frac{2}{\gamma+1},\]
\[p_*/p_0=\left(\frac{2}{\gamma+1}\right)^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)},\]
\[u_*^2=\gamma R T_* .\]

The flow through a converging exit is choked when downstream pressure is low enough for this state to be reached. Further lowering downstream pressure changes the flow after the exit; the ideal mass flux at that exit remains fixed. The model omits wall friction, heat transfer and discharge-coefficient corrections.

Worked example

Size a sonic air metering aperture

For \(p_0=200\) kPa absolute, \(T_0=300\) K, \(\gamma=1.40\) and \(R=287\) J/(kg K), the critical state has \(T_*=250\) K and \(p_*=105.7\) kPa. Then \(\rho_*=1.47\) kg/m³ and \(u_*=317\) m/s, giving \(G_*\simeq467\) kg/(m² s). A \(20.0\) mm² effective aperture meters about \(9.33\) g/s if the downstream pressure allows choking.

Second application

Decide whether a higher receiver pressure matters

Raise the receiver pressure to \(160\) kPa. Its ratio to reservoir pressure is now \(0.800\), above the critical \(0.528\). An ideal converging nozzle discharges at this pressure with \(r=0.800^{(0.4/1.4)}=0.938\). The energy equation gives \(u\approx193\) m/s, static density about \(1.98\) kg/m³, and mass flux about \(382\) kg/(m² s). Through the same aperture the predicted flow falls to \(7.64\) g/s. A model assuming choked flow at both settings would miss this change.

Account for fluid entering and leaving

A closed-system piston can be followed with its gas. A continuously operating turbine has fresh fluid crossing its boundary every second. Enclose the hardware and its internal flow in a stationary control volume. Flowing mass carries internal energy \(u\), kinetic energy \(v^2/2\) and gravitational energy \(gz\) per kilogram. Upstream pressure also does work pushing fluid in, while downstream pressure opposes its exit. Per unit mass this flow work is \(p/\rho\), motivating the specific enthalpy \(h=u+p/\rho\).

Adding all incoming and outgoing energy rates gives, for one inlet, one outlet and constant stored energy,

\[\dot Q-\dot W_s=\dot m\left[h_2-h_1+(v_2^2-v_1^2)/2+g(z_2-z_1)\right].\]

Here \(\dot W_s\) is shaft power out of the control volume. Pressure work at the flowing boundaries is already contained in enthalpy; adding it once more would count it twice. One inlet and outlet have the same steady mass rate even when their volumetric flow rates differ dramatically because gas expands.

Read the dominant energy conversion

In a short, insulated nozzle there is no shaft, the elevation difference is small and falling enthalpy raises velocity. The property \(h+v^2/2\) is then constant, often called stagnation enthalpy. In an insulated turbine with small inlet and outlet speeds, the enthalpy drop instead appears mainly as positive shaft work. An insulated compressor takes work in, raising enthalpy. An insulated throttling valve has neither shaft power nor a large net change of bulk kinetic energy between distant ports, so its inlet and outlet enthalpies are approximately equal. These components can all drop pressure during expansion, yet their energy effects differ.

For ideal-gas air with nearly constant \(c_p\), an enthalpy difference may be approximated by \(c_p\Delta T\). Keeping h in kJ/kg while using \(v^2/2\) in J/kg is a common factor-of-one-thousand error. At 100 m/s the kinetic energy is 5 kJ/kg. That estimate often decides whether an inlet velocity may be omitted from a turbine work calculation.

Evaluate heat loss and exhaust speed

A turbine passes 2.0 kg/s of gas from \(h_1=820\) to \(h_2=600\) kJ/kg. Inlet speed is 40 m/s, exhaust speed 160 m/s and the casing loses 10 kW of heat. Elevations are equal. The gas loses \(2(820-600)=440\) kW of enthalpy flow. Its kinetic-energy rate grows by \(2(160^2-40^2)/2000=24.0\) kW. The unit delivers \(440-24-10=406\) kW shaft power. Counting all 440 kW as shaft output would overstate the result by both the residual exhaust energy and the heat leak.

A separate nozzle using gas at \(h_1=820\) kJ/kg and 40 m/s could reach \(h_2=800\) kJ/kg without a shaft if its speed rose to \(\sqrt{40^2+2(20\,000)}=204\) m/s. This estimate is an energy condition. The required area ratio, pressure drop and possibility of choking also depend on compressible-flow relations.

Extend the same balance to a mixer

Two insulated water lines mix at negligible speed change. One supplies 0.20 kg/s at 360 K and the other 0.30 kg/s at 300 K. A mass balance gives 0.50 kg/s at exit. With equal, constant \(c_p\) and no shaft, summing the two inlet enthalpy rates yields an outlet temperature \([0.20(360)+0.30(300)]/0.50=324\) K. The temperature lies between the inlet values, closer to the higher mass rate. The mixer conserves energy while creating entropy. The next lecture explains how both statements can hold simultaneously and why reversing the mixer without another effect is impossible.

Interpret a measured stagnation temperature

For an insulated diffuser with negligible shaft work, reducing speed from 200 to 40 m/s raises static enthalpy by \((200^2-40^2)/2=19.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). For air with \(c_p=1.005\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\) that corresponds to a 19.1 K static temperature rise. Stagnation enthalpy \(h+V^2/2\) stays constant even if internal friction produces entropy. A stationary temperature probe that slows the arriving air may approach a stagnation reading, so comparison with a predicted static temperature requires knowing where and how the measurement was taken.

Minimise compression work by selecting an intermediate pressure

A continuous air supply can be compressed in two stages and cooled at intermediate pressure. For a steady adiabatic compressor with negligible kinetic and potential changes, shaft work input is the stream enthalpy rise. With an ideal gas, constant \(c_p,\gamma\), and a reversible stage, \(w=c_p T_{\rm in}[(p_{\rm out}/p_{\rm in})^a-1]\), where \(a=(\gamma-1)/\gamma\). This follows by integrating \(ds=c_p\,dT/T-R\,dp/p=0\), then applying \(dh=c_p\,dT\). If both stages use the same isentropic efficiency, divide each ideal rise by that efficiency.

Assume complete intercooling to the original inlet temperature \(T_1\), no pressure losses and prescribed first and last pressures. Put \(r_1=p_i/p_1,\ r_2=p_3/p_i\), so \(r_1r_2=p_3/p_1=r\). Total ideal work is \(c_pT_1[r_1^a+(r/r_1)^a-2]\). Differentiate with respect to \(\ln r_1\); the derivative is \(c_pT_1a(r_1^a-r_2^a)\). The sum has a minimum at equal ratios, giving intermediate pressure \(p_i=\sqrt{p_1p_3}\). This is a geometric mean of absolute pressures. A gauge-pressure ratio would give a completely different design.

Compare two cooled stages with a single compression

Supply air at 100 kPa and 300 K and deliver it at 900 kPa absolute. For \(\gamma=1.4,\ c_p=1.005\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\), an ideal single stage gives \(T_3=300(9^{0.2857})=562.0\,\mathrm K\) and work about 263 kJ/kg. Two equal-ratio stages operate through 300 kPa. Each reaches \(300(3^{0.2857})=410.6\,\mathrm K\) before its outlet or intercooler, requiring \(1.005(110.6)=111.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Total work is about 222.4 kJ/kg, roughly 15.5% lower. The intercooler must remove 111.2 kJ/kg between the stages under the ideal complete-cooling assumption.

With equal stage efficiencies of 0.80, each real enthalpy rise becomes 139.0 kJ/kg and the intermediate discharge temperature is about 438 K. Cooling back to 300 K then removes 139.0 kJ per kilogram, with a corresponding load of 27.8 kW at 0.20 kg/s. The equal-ratio choice survives because both stage work expressions acquired the same multiplier. An intercooler that can cool only to 330 K increases the weight of second-stage compression work; the new optimum shifts towards a larger first-stage pressure ratio. A design comparing only compressor shaft power would also need to account for coolant pumping, fan power and pressure losses before choosing the number of stages.

For an incomplete intercooler the optimal shift can be calculated. Take stage two inlet temperature \(T_m=330\) K independently maintained and identical efficiencies. The variable portion of total work is proportional to \(T_1r_1^a+T_m(r/r_1)^a\). Differentiating with respect to log first-stage ratio gives \(T_1r_1^a=T_mr_2^a\), or \(r_1/r_2=(330/300)^{1/a}=1.40\). At overall ratio nine, \(r_1=\sqrt{9(1.40)}=3.54,\ r_2=2.54\). Compressing farther while the air is colder is energetically preferable within this simplified comparison. These ratios presume the cooler can maintain 330 K over its revised duty and that both discharge temperatures stay within their allowable ranges. A compressor map may impose further flow and speed limits beyond the thermodynamic minimum.

At fixed pressure endpoints, the measured intermediate pressure alone would thus be insufficient for diagnosing whether the cooler or a compressor caused an observed power increase.

How much static temperature does a nozzle convert to speed?

Air flows steadily through an insulated nozzle with negligible shaft work and elevation change. Take \(c_p=1005\) J/(kg K). Divide the conserved stagnation enthalpy \(c_pT+u^2/2\) by \(c_p\) to find the exit temperature.
