Tutorial · resolve a damping rate to a target tolerance

For a mode well inside the grid bandwidth, estimate the number of samples per wavelength needed for a relative error below 1% in the decay rate with centred differences. Test the estimate for \(m=2\). How could the time integrator contaminate a measurement of this spatial error?

Show the resolution estimate

Writing p for samples per wavelength gives \(kh=2\pi/p\). The relative rate error is approximately \((kh)^2/12\). Requiring at most 0.01 gives \(p\gtrsim18.14\); try 19 samples per wavelength, or \(N=38\) for \(m=2\). The exact ratio \([\sin(\pi/19)/(\pi/19)]^2\) is about 0.991. A forward-Euler step would replace exponential propagation by \(1-\kappa k_{\rm eff}^2\Delta t\). Its repeated-step error could partly mask the underdamping of the spatial stencil. Compare with the exponential of the discrete symbol before attributing a measured rate to space alone.

Further reading · Lloyd N. Trefethen, Spectral Methods in MATLAB · periodic grids, Fourier differentiation and downloadable programs

Check the discrete eigenvalue with OneSimulation

A spatial sine also diagonalises the five-point Dirichlet Laplacian on a square. Use the same modified wave-number argument in both coordinate directions. Run python poisson-mode.py --mode 2 --cells 8 16 32 and compare the observed nodal error with the discrete eigenvalue prediction in the CSV. This is a steady forced problem whose spatial operator can be checked independently of any time integrator. Download the OneSimulation verification model.

Splitting a stiff relaxation from a slowly varying drive

Numerical ordinary differential equations

A sensor tries to follow a changing reference while a fast relaxation damps departures. An explicit method may oscillate wildly even though the underlying transient decays monotonically. Treating the restoring term implicitly can resolve the slow motion using steps longer than the transient time. Whether those steps measure the transient accurately is a different question.

Construct an exact benchmark with two time scales

Let \(s(t)=\sin t\) and choose a relaxation time \(\varepsilon>0\). Consider \(y'=s'(t)+(s(t)-y)/\varepsilon\), with \(y(0)=1\). Set \(e=y-s\). Subtraction of \(s'\) gives \(e'=-e/\varepsilon\), hence the reference solution \(y(t)=\sin t+e^{-t/\varepsilon}\). The slow reference changes over times of order one, while the initial mismatch decays significantly over \(\varepsilon\). We know both pieces exactly and can diagnose errors even when a stable result looks visually smooth.

Explicit Euler applied to a perturbation of the state multiplies that perturbation by \(1-r\), with \(r=h/\varepsilon\). It damps monotonically only for \(0<r\le1\), changes sign while damping for \(1<r<2\), and grows for \(r>2\). These statements compare nearby solutions at equal drive. The full signal also contains the time variation of \(s\). At the boundary \(r=2\) a state perturbation alternates without decaying. Refining the step to follow a slow input alone can therefore give the wrong choice of \(h\).

Solve only the fast part implicitly

Split \(f_{\rm slow}(t)=\cos t\) from \(f_{\rm fast}(t,y)=(\sin t-y)/\varepsilon\). Evaluate the slow part at \(t_n\) and the fast part at \(t_{n+1},y_{n+1}\). This first-order additive implicit-explicit Euler step needs one scalar linear solve:

\[(1+r)y_{n+1}=y_n+h\cos t_n+r\sin t_{n+1}.\]

The state perturbation now multiplies by \(1/(1+r)\). It is damped at every positive step and tends to zero as \(r\) grows. Subtract \(\sin t_{n+1}\) from both sides after solving. The mismatch obeys \(e_{n+1}=[e_n+h\cos t_n-(\sin t_{n+1}-\sin t_n)]/(1+r)\). The bracketed forcing defect is \(O(h^2)\) for smooth input. In the stiff limit at fixed \(h\), the endpoint approaches the current reference. Moving the reference in the fast term to the old time would instead drag it one sample behind in that limit. The choice of what to treat implicitly includes the time at which a fast equilibrium is imposed.

Test early-time accuracy and late-time stability

For \(\varepsilon=0.02\), \(h=0.05\), \(r=2.5\). At the first step the exact value is \(\sin(0.05)+e^{-2.5}=0.1321\). Explicit Euler gives \(1+0.05(1-50)=-1.45\). The implicit-explicit value is \([1+0.05+2.5\sin(0.05)]/3.5=0.3357\). The latter is stable but visibly overestimates the surviving transient. Its damping factor \(1/3.5=0.286\) differs substantially from \(e^{-2.5}=0.0821\). A question about peak rate or initial settling requires shorter steps even if the late-time reference is recovered.

The experiment holds the final time at one while the number of steps changes. The vertical coordinate is the base-ten logarithm of absolute error, with values below \(10^{-15}\) drawn at that floor. Negative heights therefore correspond to errors less than one. Track the maximum error separately from the final error as the transient dies out. Try an initially unstable explicit run, then double the number of steps. The stiffness criterion depends on the ratio \(h/\varepsilon\), while accuracy on the reference also depends on \(h\) itself.

Tutorial on identifying which component is stiff

Keep \(h=0.04\) and reduce \(\varepsilon\) from 0.04 to 0.004. For each case compare exact, explicit and implicit amplification of a state perturbation. What quantity would force a reduction of \(h\) even with the implicit-explicit method?

Work through the solution

At \(r=1\) the three factors are \(e^{-1}=0.368\), zero and \(1/2\). At \(r=10\) they are \(4.54\times10^{-5}\), \(-9\) and \(1/11=0.0909\). For a measurement of the initial decay rate the implicit discretisation substantially misrepresents both exact factors. Bounding error during that transient requires smaller steps relative to \(\varepsilon\), even though its numerical amplification is stable.

Further reading · Ascher, Ruuth and Wetton, implicit-explicit time discretisations.

Learning a drifting temperature from a sequence of readings

Inverse problems and least squares

Derive the scalar Kalman update by completing the square and see how process uncertainty controls memory of the past.

Propagate a distribution through time

A drifting offset follows \(x_k=x_{k-1}+w_k\). A thermometer reports \(z_k=x_k+v_k\). Assume independent, zero-mean Gaussian process increments of variance \(Q\) and sensor noise of variance \(R\), independent at each time and of the initial state. Before the next reading, the mean stays at \(m^-=m_{k-1}\) and the variance adds, \(P^-=P_{k-1}+Q\).

The negative log posterior, up to constants, is \((x-m^-)^2/(2P^-)+(z_k-x)^2/(2R)\). Collect the coefficient of \(x^2\) and its linear term. The result can be written in two equivalent forms:

\[P_k=(1/P^-+1/R)^{-1},\]
\[m_k=m^-+K(z_k-m^-),\]
\[K=P^-/(P^-+R).\]

Thus the fraction of the innovation used is a model prediction. When sensors are noisy the gain falls. When the model permits rapid drift, uncertainty grows between readings, giving the new measurement a larger role. These are variances, so a stated standard deviation must be squared first.

A reading with units and a repeated reading

A prior says \(20.0\pm2.0\) °C (one standard deviation), with negligible intervening drift. A sensor of standard deviation 1 °C reads 23 °C. The gain is \(4/(4+1)=0.8\), giving \(22.4\) °C and variance \(0.8\) °C². A second independent reading of 23 °C gives gain \(0.8/1.8\) and mean \(22.6667\) °C. Treating a duplicate record as independent would falsely claim the same information gain.

In a long random walk with \(Q=0.25\) and \(R=1\) in squared units, a steady posterior variance satisfies \(P=(P+Q)R/(P+Q+R)\). Rearrangement gives \(P^2+QP-QR=0\), so the physically positive root is about 0.3904. New uncertainty replenishes the information squeezed out by measurement.

Follow a change in the underlying temperature

For a reproducible illustration, a fixed synthetic thermometer trace contains a smooth periodic disturbance and a step in the hidden temperature. It is deliberately held fixed while the filter's assumed \(Q\) and \(R\) change. Compare the lag just after the step with the wiggle once the temperature settles.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on noisy data over time

If \(Q=0\), show how \(n\) independent readings with the same variance affect precision.

Worked solution

Each update adds \(1/R\) to precision \(1/P\). Induction gives \(1/P_n=1/P_0+n/R\). This is precisely the precision of a batch Gaussian update.

What pattern in the innovations would call the random-walk assumptions into question?

Worked solution

A long run of positive innovations after a systematic ramp suggests bias in the prediction. Serial correlation suggests missing dynamics or correlated observation errors. Check against predicted innovation variance \(P^-+R\) and examine signs and lags as well as their sizes.

Read the university treatment

Extend the scalar precision update to several state variables and identify the matrix made invertible by a positive observation variance. Stephen Boyd · Stanford EE363, estimation and the Kalman filter

Predicting a new measurement when the fitted parameters are correlated

Inverse problems and least squares

Propagate the full posterior covariance to a quantity of interest, distinguish latent and measured uncertainty and select the observation that reduces the uncertainty of the actual prediction.

One model supplies many possible targets

For parameters \(\theta\) with posterior mean \(m\) and covariance \(C\), a linear target \(s=h^T\theta\) has mean \(h^Tm\). Subtract the mean and expand the quadratic product to obtain \(\operatorname{Var}(s)=h^TCh\). An independent sensor with variance \(R\) changes the variance of a future measured value to \(h^TCh+R\). Repeated fits of noisy datasets and repeated physical measurements are distinct sources of variation.

A candidate new observation \(z=a^T\theta+\epsilon\) with variance \(R_a\) has covariance \(h^TCa\) with the target. For a Gaussian posterior the conditional covariance update is \(C_+=C-Caa^TC/(a^TCa+R_a)\), found either by completing the square or projecting out the optimal multiple of the innovation. The target variance decreases by

\[\Delta\operatorname{Var}(s)=\frac{(h^TCa)^2}{a^TCa+R_a}.\]

A precise sum and an uncertain difference

Let \(C\) have diagonal entries 1 and correlation \(-0.9\). A target \(h=(1,1)\) has variance \(2-1.8=0.2\). The difference \(h=(1,-1)\) has variance 3.8. Ignoring correlations would predict 2 for both. With sensor variance 0.25, a newly measured sum has variance 0.45 although the latent sum variance remains 0.2.

For the target sum, measuring the first coordinate with variance 0.25 reduces variance by \((1-0.9)^2/(1+0.25)=0.008\). Measuring the sum itself at the same variance reduces it by \(0.2^2/(0.2+0.25)=0.0889\). These two sensors have differently normalised sensitivity vectors. Their stated noise levels refer to their own output units and must be calibrated before a cost comparison.

Choose the measurement direction

For a fairer geometric comparison, restrict both measurement and prediction directions to unit length. Fix the target along the normalised sum. Sweep candidate observation angles and read the remaining prediction variance. Change the parameter correlation: when does a coordinate sensor improve the target appreciably?

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on prediction and parameter recovery

If \(h^TCa=0\), can a noise-free measurement of \(a^T\theta\) improve the linear prediction under these Gaussian assumptions?

Worked solution

The covariance reduction is exactly zero. The measurement may determine another direction precisely while carrying no linear information about the target.

The posterior on \(\theta\) is strongly non-Gaussian. Which conclusions still hold exactly?

Worked solution

Mean and variance of a fixed linear prediction still follow from its first two moments, and independent sensor variance still adds. The Gaussian conditional update and an interval based only on two standard deviations need additional assumptions.

Read the university treatment

Compare the projection of a vector measurement with the covariance of a scalar target. Check the direction of each matrix product. Stephen Boyd · Stanford EE363, estimation

Applied study

Connect heat exchange and thermal equilibrium to energy conservation, entropy and microstates.

Which temperature will two bodies reach?

Touch two metal blocks together inside an insulated container. Their final temperature is constrained by conservation of energy. That law alone also permits the hot block to heat up at the expense of the cold one. A second idea, entropy, selects the direction we actually observe. A third, thermal conductance, sets the time it takes.

Assume that each block is internally close to equilibrium and has a constant total heat capacity \(C_i\) in J/K. The blocks exchange heat but do no external work. A transfer \(dQ\) from block 1 to block 2 produces \(C_1dT_1=-dQ\) and \(C_2dT_2=dQ\). Adding and integrating gives

\[C_1T_1+C_2T_2=\text{constant},\qquad T_*=\frac{C_1T_{1,0}+C_2T_{2,0}}{C_1+C_2}.\]

The second expression assumes the bodies have reached a common temperature. A larger heat capacity puts more weight on that block’s starting temperature.

Count the ways to share energy

At fixed total energy \(U\), suppose block 1 has energy \(U_1\). Weakly coupled blocks have \(\Omega_1(U_1)\Omega_2(U-U_1)\) accessible combinations of microscopic states. For an isolated equilibrium system with equally likely microstates, this multiplicity determines the probability of an energy partition. Taking \(S=k_B\ln\Omega\) turns the product into a sum. At its maximum

\[\frac{dS_{\rm total}}{dU_1}=\frac{1}{T_1}-\frac{1}{T_2}=0,\qquad \frac{1}{T_i}=\frac{\partial S_i}{\partial U_i}.\]

Positive heat capacities make the curvature negative, since \(\partial^2S_i/\partial U_i^2=-1/(C_iT_i^2)\). Equal temperature thus maximises total entropy. Before equilibrium a small transfer from hot to cold increases entropy by \(dQ(1/T_{\rm cold}-1/T_{\rm hot})>0\). Temperatures here must be absolute.

From an isolated pair to a canonical ensemble

When a small system contacts a much larger reservoir, its temperature is effectively set by the reservoir. The chance of observing a particular microstate of energy \(E_j\) is proportional to the number of reservoir states at energy \(U-E_j\). Expand the reservoir entropy about \(U\): \(S_R(U-E_j)\simeq S_R(U)-E_j/T\). Since multiplicity is exponential in entropy, the probability becomes \(p_j=e^{-\beta E_j}/Z\), with \(\beta=1/(k_BT)\) and \(Z=\sum_j e^{-\beta E_j}\).

For one unit with levels \(0\) and \(\Delta\), \(Z=1+e^{-\beta\Delta}\). If \(\Delta=k_BT\ln 3\), the excited state has probability \(1/4\) and mean energy is \(\Delta/4\). Raising temperature at fixed gap drives the two probabilities toward one half. This microscopic account relates heat capacity to the response of mean energy to temperature.

Same change of state, different heat and work

For a closed system the first law reads \(dU=\delta Q-\delta W\), with work positive when the system does it. Internal energy and entropy are state functions. Heat and work depend on the path. To see this, compare two ways for one mole of ideal gas initially at 300 K to expand to twice its volume.

During reversible isothermal expansion, \(p=RT/V\) and \(W=\int p\,dV=RT\ln2=1.73\) kJ. The ideal gas’s internal energy depends only on temperature, so \(Q=W\). Free expansion into an evacuated chamber inside an insulated shell instead gives \(W=Q=0\). The final temperature is again 300 K. Both paths give the same gas entropy increase \(R\ln2=5.76\) J/K. In the reversible case the reservoir loses this much entropy, while in free expansion the insulated gas produces it internally. In the two-block problem the entropy production likewise measures irreversibility inside the chosen boundary.

Give the contact a conductance

Model a short thermal bridge by a constant conductance \(G\) in W/K with negligible heat storage. Its heat flow is \(\dot Q=G(T_1-T_2)\). Subtract the two heat balances to obtain \(d(T_1-T_2)/dt=-G(1/C_1+1/C_2)(T_1-T_2)\). The difference decays exponentially with \(\tau=C_1C_2/[G(C_1+C_2)]\). Meanwhile the entropy production rate is \(G(T_1-T_2)^2/(T_1T_2)\), which falls to zero at equilibrium.

The equilibrium temperature is independent of the bridge conductance, provided heat transfer can continue. The same energy balance for a body in contact with a reservoir gives a different time constant because the reservoir temperature stays fixed. Cooling a body in a room.

A hot block and a larger cold block

Take \(C_1=500\) J/K at 360 K, \(C_2=1500\) J/K at 300 K and \(G=2.0\) W/K. The common equilibrium temperature is \(315\) K. Block 1 eventually releases \(500(360-315)=22.5\) kJ and block 2 absorbs \(1500(315-300)=22.5\) kJ. The initial heat current is 120 W and \(\tau=187.5\) s.

To find the entropy change between the initial and final states, integrate \(dS=C\,dT/T\) for each block along a reversible reference path. The sum is \(500\ln(315/360)+1500\ln(315/300)=6.42\) J/K. Energy transfer cancels between the two bodies. Their entropy changes have different magnitudes because they receive or lose heat at different temperatures.

Exercises on microscopic and macroscopic equilibrium

1. Count a small system by hand

Two groups each contain four distinguishable two-level units. Distribute four identical excitation quanta over the eight units, at most one quantum per unit, with all microstates equally likely. Which partition is most probable? Does it occur every time?

Count the microscopic arrangements

If group 1 has \(j\) quanta, the multiplicity is \(\binom4j\binom4{4-j}\). For \(j=0,1,2,3,4\) this gives \(1,16,36,16,1\), summing to 70. Equal sharing is the most probable partition, with probability \(36/70\). A small system shows substantial fluctuations around this partition. In macroscopic systems the relative spread of the energy share is typically much smaller.

2. Diagnose an energy-conserving prediction

From the worked initial state a proposed model raises the hot block by 3 K and lowers the colder block by 1 K. Test its energy and entropy changes for an isolated, passive pair.

Apply both thermodynamic laws

The net energy change is \(500(3)+1500(-1)=0\). But \(500\ln(363/360)+1500\ln(299/300)=-0.86\) J/K to two decimals. The macroscopic change contradicts the second law for a passive isolated pair. An external device moving heat uphill would require a larger system boundary to account for its operation.

OneSimulation laboratory

Separate the equilibrium temperature from the relaxation time

Compare recorded OneSimulation runs on this page. To test your own inputs, download the notebook and model files and run the model in an activated OneSimulation environment.

The notebook couples two thermal capacities through their temperature inputs. Run its parameter, solve and comparison cells for each setting. The log-slope of \(T_1-T_2\) provides a measured decay rate. Its inverse is the measured relaxation time. Use the capacity-weighted mean at every stored time to estimate \(T_*\), since the two temperatures still differ at the final stored sample. Two-body thermal exchange model.

  1. Baseline. Start with heat capacities of 500 and 1500 J/K, initial temperatures of 360 and 300 K, and a 2 W/K bridge. Compare the time for the initial temperature difference to fall by a factor \(e\) with 187.5 s. Check entropy by calculating \(C_1\ln(T_1/T_{1,0})+C_2\ln(T_2/T_{2,0})\) across the stored history.
  2. Improve the bridge. Change conductance from 2 to 4 W/K and rerun. Predict which of the weighted mean and decay rate will double.
  3. Change the hot body. Restore the conductance and set heat_capacity_1 = 1500.0 J/K. Examine how the fitted rate and weighted mean both change. For each run compare the fitted rate with \(1/\tau\) within 0.1%, and check that the weighted mean drifts by less than 0.001 K.
